Um… laxatives or diuretics? I don’t really… I’m not sure. I mean, I know what laxatives are, but diuretics, is that like water pills? My aunt used to talk about those all the time because her ankles would swell, and she’d keep them in one of those little pill organizers on the kitchen counter.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Most weeks, I think. Like… it’s not every day, but for the last few months it’s been pretty regular—maybe once or twice a week I’ll have a night where I eat way more than I meant to.

And then the next day I kind of go into this “fix it” mode. I’ll skip breakfast, sometimes lunch, or I’ll make myself exercise longer than I planned. Like if I was only going to walk a bit, I’ll turn it into this whole long thing. It’s almost like I’m keeping score with myself, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what it feels like.

My aunt used to talk about those all the time because her ankles would swell, and she’d keep them in one of those little pill organizers on the kitchen counter.\n\nFor me, after I eat too much, I mostly just feel uncomfortable and mad at myself, and then I start thinking about what I have to do the next morning—like whether I need to be in front of clients or if I can hide at my desk a little. I can’t say I’ve noticed a pattern with taking something like that afterward, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, and then you don't do anything to try to undo the effects of that eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… usually I do something, just not always right then. Like the next day I’ll tell myself I’m “making up for it,” you know? I’ll skip breakfast, and then lunch too sometimes, or I’ll make myself take this extra-long hike even if my legs are tired. \n\nAnd with softball conditioning, I’ll push harder than I probably need to, because in my head it balances things out somehow. I know that sounds a little… I don’t know, like a bargain I’m making with myself. But it doesn’t always feel like I’m doing something dramatic. It’s more like, “Okay, yesterday was bad, so today you have to be stricter.”\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you planned happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks lately. Like, not every single day or anything, but maybe one or two nights in a week where I look back and think, “That was way more than I meant to eat.”\n\nIt’s been going on for a few months, I think. I lose track a little because weeks blur together, especially with work and appointments and all that. But yeah, usually after one of those nights, the next day I’m trying to make up for it somehow, like skipping meals or making myself exercise longer than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: How often do you do something to make up for eating more than you planned, like skipping meals or exercising more?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I think. Like… it’s not every day, but for the last few months it’s been pretty regular—maybe once or twice a week I’ll have a night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day I kind of go into this “fix it” mode. I’ll skip breakfast, sometimes lunch, or I’ll make myself exercise longer than I planned. Like if I was only going to walk a bit, I’ll turn it into this whole long thing. It’s almost like I’m keeping score with myself, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. It’s not something I’ve really kept track of or talked about much, and I get embarrassed when it gets that specific.\n\nI guess when you ask it that directly, my mind kind of goes blank. Like I start thinking about whether I’m saying it “right,” which I know isn’t the point. And if I’ve been really “good” all day, like keeping things strict, then some evenings I end up eating way more than I planned. It’ll start with dinner, and then somehow it’s cereal, crackers, leftovers, sweets—just sort of one thing after another over a couple hours. I keep telling myself I’ll get back on track tomorrow, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight when you think about these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not always in a big dramatic way, but it gets in my head. Like if I’m getting ready for a client luncheon or one of those leadership meetings and my waistband feels tight, I start thinking I look sloppy, like people won’t take me as seriously. Which is silly, because I know I’m still doing my job fine, and honestly half the time everyone’s just worried about their own numbers or whatever. But I’ll be standing there tugging at my clothes and thinking about how I look instead of what I’m supposed to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve felt awful after, like too full and embarrassed, and then I’ll start cleaning the kitchen or checking emails because I don’t want to think about it. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m going to be more disciplined the next day, especially if I’ve got appointments or I have to dress up for work. But as far as making myself vomit… I don’t know, I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: Um… laxatives or diuretics? I don’t really… I’m not sure. I mean, I know what laxatives are, but diuretics, is that like water pills? My aunt used to talk about those all the time because her ankles would swell, and she’d keep them in one of those little pill organizers on the kitchen counter.\n\nFor me, after I eat too much, I mostly just feel uncomfortable and mad at myself, and then I start thinking about what I have to do the next morning—like whether I need to be in front of clients or if I can hide at my desk a little. I can’t say I’ve noticed a pattern with taking something like that afterward, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, and then you don't do anything to try to undo the effects of that eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… usually I do something, just not always right then. Like the next day I’ll tell myself I’m “making up for it,” you know? I’ll skip breakfast, and then lunch too sometimes, or I’ll make myself take this extra-long hike even if my legs are tired. \n\nAnd with softball conditioning, I’ll push harder than I probably need to, because in my head it balances things out somehow. I know that sounds a little… I don’t know, like a bargain I’m making with myself. But it doesn’t always feel like I’m doing something dramatic. It’s more like, “Okay, yesterday was bad, so today you have to be stricter.”\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you planned happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks lately. Like, not every single day or anything, but maybe one or two nights in a week where I look back and think, “That was way more than I meant to eat.”\n\nIt’s been going on for a few months, I think. I lose track a little because weeks blur together, especially with work and appointments and all that. But yeah, usually after one of those nights, the next day I’m trying to make up for it somehow, like skipping meals or making myself exercise longer than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: How often do you do something to make up for eating more than you planned, like skipping meals or exercising more?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I think. Like… it’s not every day, but for the last few months it’s been pretty regular—maybe once or twice a week I’ll have a night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day I kind of go into this “fix it” mode. I’ll skip breakfast, sometimes lunch, or I’ll make myself exercise longer than I planned. Like if I was only going to walk a bit, I’ll turn it into this whole long thing. It’s almost like I’m keeping score with myself, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. It’s not something I’ve really kept track of or talked about much, and I get embarrassed when it gets that specific.\n\nI guess when you ask it that directly, my mind kind of goes blank. Like I start thinking about whether I’m saying it “right,” which I know isn’t the point. I’m sorry—I might need you to ask it again a different way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, after those nights I feel awful and I’ll start thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then my brain jumps to all these rules about the next day.\n\nThe vomiting part… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep track of things that way, and honestly I get kind of embarrassed even talking about it. Like when my daughter asks if I’m okay after dinner, I’ll just say I’m tired, because who wants to get into all that at the kitchen table, you know?\n\nSo… I guess I’m not giving you a yes-or-no. I’m sorry. I just don’t know how to say it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It’s more like, “Okay, yesterday was bad, so today you have to be stricter.”\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you planned happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks lately. Like, not every single day or anything, but maybe one or two nights in a week where I look back and think, “That was way more than I meant to eat.”\n\nIt’s been going on for a few months, I think. I lose track a little because weeks blur together, especially with work and appointments and all that. But yeah, usually after one of those nights, the next day I’m trying to make up for it somehow, like skipping meals or making myself exercise longer than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: How often do you do something to make up for eating more than you planned, like skipping meals or exercising more?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I think. Like… it’s not every day, but for the last few months it’s been pretty regular—maybe once or twice a week I’ll have a night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day I kind of go into this “fix it” mode. I’ll skip breakfast, sometimes lunch, or I’ll make myself exercise longer than I planned. Like if I was only going to walk a bit, I’ll turn it into this whole long thing. It’s almost like I’m keeping score with myself, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. It’s not something I’ve really kept track of or talked about much, and I get embarrassed when it gets that specific.\n\nI guess when you ask it that directly, my mind kind of goes blank. Like I start thinking about whether I’m saying it “right,” which I know isn’t the point. I’m sorry—I might need you to ask it again a different way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, after those nights I feel awful and I’ll start thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then my brain jumps to all these rules about the next day.\n\nThe vomiting part… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep track of things that way, and honestly I get kind of embarrassed even talking about it. Like when my daughter asks if I’m okay after dinner, I’ll just say I’m tired, because who wants to get into all that at the kitchen table, you know?\n\nSo… I guess I’m not giving you a yes-or-no. I’m sorry. I just don’t know how to say it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after eating more than you planned, and if so, how often does it happen?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a straight answer. I just… I don’t have a number for that. It’s not something I’ve sat down and counted, and when I try to think about it I start remembering random things, like whether my husband was home or if I had work the next morning, instead of the actual answer.\n\nAfter I eat more than I meant to, I mostly just feel ashamed and want the whole thing to be over. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s my eating, mostly. I work in insurance sales, so by the time I get home some days I’m just… worn out, you know? And if I’ve been really “good” all day, like keeping things strict, then some evenings I end up eating way more than I planned. It’ll start with dinner, and then somehow it’s cereal, crackers, leftovers, sweets—just sort of one thing after another over a couple hours. I keep telling myself I’ll get back on track tomorrow, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight when you think about these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not always in a big dramatic way, but it gets in my head. Like if I’m getting ready for a client luncheon or one of those leadership meetings and my waistband feels tight, I start thinking I look sloppy, like people won’t take me as seriously. Which is silly, because I know I’m still doing my job fine, and honestly half the time everyone’s just worried about their own numbers or whatever. But I’ll be standing there tugging at my clothes and thinking about how I look instead of what I’m supposed to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve felt awful after, like too full and embarrassed, and then I’ll start cleaning the kitchen or checking emails because I don’t want to think about it. But as far as making myself vomit… I don’t know, I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: Um… laxatives or diuretics? I don’t really… I’m not sure. I mean, I know what laxatives are, but diuretics, is that like water pills? My aunt used to talk about those all the time because her ankles would swell, and she’d keep them in one of those little pill organizers on the kitchen counter.\n\nFor me, after I eat too much, I mostly just feel uncomfortable and mad at myself, and then I start thinking about what I have to do the next morning—like whether I need to be in front of clients or if I can hide at my desk a little. Like when my daughter asks if I’m okay after dinner, I’ll just say I’m tired, because who wants to get into all that at the kitchen table, you know?\n\nSo… I guess I’m not giving you a yes-or-no. I just… I don’t have a number for that. It’s not something I’ve sat down and counted, and when I try to think about it I start remembering random things, like whether my husband was home or if I had work the next morning, instead of the actual answer.\n\nAfter I eat more than I meant to, I mostly just feel ashamed and want the whole thing to be over. But saying exactly whether I made myself vomit, and how often… I’m not sure I can answer that clearly right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I keep getting stuck on the “made myself” part, I think. Like, after eating too much, I definitely feel upset and uncomfortable, and then I kind of go into my head about it.\n\nBut whether I’ve actually done that after those episodes… I don’t know. I know that sounds evasive. It’s just not something I have a clear answer for right now. My mind goes to all the circumstances around it—like if everyone was asleep, or if I had to get up early for work—instead of giving you the simple yes or no you’re asking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clear yes. I mean, after I eat more than I wanted to, I get really upset with myself, and then I start thinking about everything else—what I have to do the next morning, whether anyone noticed, whether I can just move on and pretend it didn’t happen.\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge you. I just don’t have it organized in my head like that, whether I’ve made myself vomit or how to describe it. It all gets kind of blurry when I try to talk about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.4, F50.5\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s my eating, mostly. I work in insurance sales, so by the time I get home some days I’m just… worn out, you know? And if I’ve been really “good” all day, like keeping things strict, then some evenings I end up eating way more than I planned. It’ll start with dinner, and then somehow it’s cereal, crackers, leftovers, sweets—just sort of one thing after another over a couple hours. I keep telling myself I’ll get back on track tomorrow, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight when you think about these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not always in a big dramatic way, but it gets in my head. Like if I’m getting ready for a client luncheon or one of those leadership meetings and my waistband feels tight, I start thinking I look sloppy, like people won’t take me as seriously. Which is silly, because I know I’m still doing my job fine, and honestly half the time everyone’s just worried about their own numbers or whatever. But I’ll be standing there tugging at my clothes and thinking about how I look instead of what I’m supposed to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve felt awful after, like too full and embarrassed, and then I’ll start cleaning the kitchen or checking emails because I don’t want to think about it. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m going to be more disciplined the next day, especially if I’ve got appointments or I have to dress up for work. But as far as making myself vomit… I don’t know, I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: Um… laxatives or diuretics? I don’t really… I’m not sure. I mean, I know what laxatives are, but diuretics, is that like water pills? My aunt used to talk about those all the time because her ankles would swell, and she’d keep them in one of those little pill organizers on the kitchen counter.\n\nFor me, after I eat too much, I mostly just feel uncomfortable and mad at myself, and then I start thinking about what I have to do the next morning—like whether I need to be in front of clients or if I can hide at my desk a little. I can’t say I’ve noticed a pattern with taking something like that afterward, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, and then you don't do anything to try to undo the effects of that eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… usually I do something, just not always right then. Like the next day I’ll tell myself I’m “making up for it,” you know? I’ll skip breakfast, and then lunch too sometimes, or I’ll make myself take this extra-long hike even if my legs are tired. \n\nAnd with softball conditioning, I’ll push harder than I probably need to, because in my head it balances things out somehow. I know that sounds a little… I don’t know, like a bargain I’m making with myself. But it doesn’t always feel like I’m doing something dramatic. It’s more like, “Okay, yesterday was bad, so today you have to be stricter.”\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you planned happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks lately. Like, not every single day or anything, but maybe one or two nights in a week where I look back and think, “That was way more than I meant to eat.”\n\nIt’s been going on for a few months, I think. I lose track a little because weeks blur together, especially with work and appointments and all that. But yeah, usually after one of those nights, the next day I’m trying to make up for it somehow, like skipping meals or making myself exercise longer than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: How often do you do something to make up for eating more than you planned, like skipping meals or exercising more?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I think. Like… it’s not every day, but for the last few months it’s been pretty regular—maybe once or twice a week I’ll have a night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day I kind of go into this “fix it” mode. I’ll skip breakfast, sometimes lunch, or I’ll make myself exercise longer than I planned. Like if I was only going to walk a bit, I’ll turn it into this whole long thing. It’s almost like I’m keeping score with myself, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. It’s not something I’ve really kept track of or talked about much, and I get embarrassed when it gets that specific.\n\nI guess when you ask it that directly, my mind kind of goes blank. Like I start thinking about whether I’m saying it “right,” which I know isn’t the point. I’m sorry—I might need you to ask it again a different way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, after those nights I feel awful and I’ll start thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then my brain jumps to all these rules about the next day.\n\nThe vomiting part… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep track of things that way, and honestly I get kind of embarrassed even talking about it. Like when my daughter asks if I’m okay after dinner, I’ll just say I’m tired, because who wants to get into all that at the kitchen table, you know?\n\nSo… I guess I’m not giving you a yes-or-no. I don’t really keep track of things that way, and honestly I get kind of embarrassed even talking about it. Like when my daughter asks if I’m okay after dinner, I’ll just say I’m tired, because who wants to get into all that at the kitchen table, you know?\n\nSo… I guess I’m not giving you a yes-or-no. I’m sorry. I just don’t know how to say it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after eating more than you planned, and if so, how often does it happen?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a straight answer. I just… I don’t have a number for that. It’s not something I’ve sat down and counted, and when I try to think about it I start remembering random things, like whether my husband was home or if I had work the next morning, instead of the actual answer.\n\nAfter I eat more than I meant to, I mostly just feel ashamed and want the whole thing to be over. But saying exactly whether I made myself vomit, and how often… I’m not sure I can answer that clearly right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I keep getting stuck on the “made myself” part, I think. Like, after eating too much, I definitely feel upset and uncomfortable, and then I kind of go into my head about it.\n\nBut whether I’ve actually done that after those episodes… I don’t know. I know that sounds evasive. It’s just not something I have a clear answer for right now. My mind goes to all the circumstances around it—like if everyone was asleep, or if I had to get up early for work—instead of giving you the simple yes or no you’re asking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clear yes. I mean, after I eat more than I wanted to, I get really upset with myself, and then I start thinking about everything else—what I have to do the next morning, whether anyone noticed, whether I can just move on and pretend it didn’t happen.\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge you. I just don’t have it organized in my head like that, whether I’ve made myself vomit or how to describe it. It all gets kind of blurry when I try to talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever intentionally made yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. “Intentionally” makes me feel like I’m supposed to know exactly what I was deciding in the moment, and honestly, when I’ve eaten too much I’m usually just upset and sort of embarrassed.\n\nSometimes I think more about the situation around it—like whether I had to be at work early, or if I could just go to bed and pretend it didn’t happen—than about the actual question you’re asking. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s my eating, mostly. I work in insurance sales, so by the time I get home some days I’m just… worn out, you know? It’ll start with dinner, and then somehow it’s cereal, crackers, leftovers, sweets—just sort of one thing after another over a couple hours. I keep telling myself I’ll get back on track tomorrow, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight when you think about these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not always in a big dramatic way, but it gets in my head. Like if I’m getting ready for a client luncheon or one of those leadership meetings and my waistband feels tight, I start thinking I look sloppy, like people won’t take me as seriously. Which is silly, because I know I’m still doing my job fine, and honestly half the time everyone’s just worried about their own numbers or whatever. But I’ll be standing there tugging at my clothes and thinking about how I look instead of what I’m supposed to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve felt awful after, like too full and embarrassed, and then I’ll start cleaning the kitchen or checking emails because I don’t want to think about it. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m going to be more disciplined the next day, especially if I’ve got appointments or I have to dress up for work. But as far as making myself vomit… I don’t know, I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: Um… laxatives or diuretics? I don’t really… I’m not sure. I mean, I know what laxatives are, but diuretics, is that like water pills? My aunt used to talk about those all the time because her ankles would swell, and she’d keep them in one of those little pill organizers on the kitchen counter.\n\nFor me, after I eat too much, I mostly just feel uncomfortable and mad at myself, and then I start thinking about what I have to do the next morning—like whether I need to be in front of clients or if I can hide at my desk a little. I can’t say I’ve noticed a pattern with taking something like that afterward, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, and then you don't do anything to try to undo the effects of that eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… usually I do something, just not always right then. It’s more like, “Okay, yesterday was bad, so today you have to be stricter.”\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you planned happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks lately. I’ll skip breakfast, sometimes lunch, or I’ll make myself exercise longer than I planned. Like if I was only going to walk a bit, I’ll turn it into this whole long thing. It’s almost like I’m keeping score with myself, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. It’s not something I’ve really kept track of or talked about much, and I get embarrassed when it gets that specific.\n\nI guess when you ask it that directly, my mind kind of goes blank. Like I start thinking about whether I’m saying it “right,” which I know isn’t the point. I’m sorry—I might need you to ask it again a different way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, after those nights I feel awful and I’ll start thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then my brain jumps to all these rules about the next day.\n\nThe vomiting part… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep track of things that way, and honestly I get kind of embarrassed even talking about it. Like when my daughter asks if I’m okay after dinner, I’ll just say I’m tired, because who wants to get into all that at the kitchen table, you know?\n\nSo… I guess I’m not giving you a yes-or-no. I’m sorry. I just don’t know how to say it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after eating more than you planned, and if so, how often does it happen?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a straight answer. I just… I don’t have a number for that. It’s not something I’ve sat down and counted, and when I try to think about it I start remembering random things, like whether my husband was home or if I had work the next morning, instead of the actual answer.\n\nAfter I eat more than I meant to, I mostly just feel ashamed and want the whole thing to be over. But saying exactly whether I made myself vomit, and how often… I’m not sure I can answer that clearly right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I keep getting stuck on the “made myself” part, I think. Like, after eating too much, I definitely feel upset and uncomfortable, and then I kind of go into my head about it.\n\nBut whether I’ve actually done that after those episodes… I don’t know. I know that sounds evasive. It’s just not something I have a clear answer for right now. My mind goes to all the circumstances around it—like if everyone was asleep, or if I had to get up early for work—instead of giving you the simple yes or no you’re asking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clear yes. I mean, after I eat more than I wanted to, I get really upset with myself, and then I start thinking about everything else—what I have to do the next morning, whether anyone noticed, whether I can just move on and pretend it didn’t happen.\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge you. I just don’t have it organized in my head like that, whether I’ve made myself vomit or how to describe it. It all gets kind of blurry when I try to talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever intentionally made yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. “Intentionally” makes me feel like I’m supposed to know exactly what I was deciding in the moment, and honestly, when I’ve eaten too much I’m usually just upset and sort of embarrassed.\n\nSometimes I think more about the situation around it—like whether I had to be at work early, or if I could just go to bed and pretend it didn’t happen—than about the actual question you’re asking. So I can’t honestly give you a clear yes or no right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything else to try to undo the effects of eating more than you planned, besides exercising more or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. When you say “undo,” I get what you mean, but my mind kind of goes to, like, trying to fix the day in general—cleaning up the kitchen, making plans for the next morning, telling myself I’ll be more normal tomorrow.\n\nI don’t know if there’s something specific you’re asking about besides eating less or moving more. I can’t think of anything clear right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. My aunt used to talk about those all the time because her ankles would swell, and she’d keep them in one of those little pill organizers on the kitchen counter.\n\nFor me, after I eat too much, I mostly just feel uncomfortable and mad at myself, and then I start thinking about what I have to do the next morning—like whether I need to be in front of clients or if I can hide at my desk a little. I can’t say I’ve noticed a pattern with taking something like that afterward, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, and then you don't do anything to try to undo the effects of that eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… usually I do something, just not always right then. Like the next day I’ll tell myself I’m “making up for it,” you know? I’ll skip breakfast, and then lunch too sometimes, or I’ll make myself take this extra-long hike even if my legs are tired. \n\nAnd with softball conditioning, I’ll push harder than I probably need to, because in my head it balances things out somehow. I know that sounds a little… I don’t know, like a bargain I’m making with myself. But it doesn’t always feel like I’m doing something dramatic. It’s more like, “Okay, yesterday was bad, so today you have to be stricter.”\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you planned happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks lately. Like, not every single day or anything, but maybe one or two nights in a week where I look back and think, “That was way more than I meant to eat.”\n\nIt’s been going on for a few months, I think. I lose track a little because weeks blur together, especially with work and appointments and all that. But yeah, usually after one of those nights, the next day I’m trying to make up for it somehow, like skipping meals or making myself exercise longer than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: How often do you do something to make up for eating more than you planned, like skipping meals or exercising more?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I think. Like… it’s not every day, but for the last few months it’s been pretty regular—maybe once or twice a week I’ll have a night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day I kind of go into this “fix it” mode. I’ll skip breakfast, sometimes lunch, or I’ll make myself exercise longer than I planned. Like if I was only going to walk a bit, I’ll turn it into this whole long thing. It’s almost like I’m keeping score with myself, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. It’s not something I’ve really kept track of or talked about much, and I get embarrassed when it gets that specific.\n\nI guess when you ask it that directly, my mind kind of goes blank. Like I start thinking about whether I’m saying it “right,” which I know isn’t the point. I’m sorry—I might need you to ask it again a different way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, after those nights I feel awful and I’ll start thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then my brain jumps to all these rules about the next day.\n\nThe vomiting part… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep track of things that way, and honestly I get kind of embarrassed even talking about it. Like when my daughter asks if I’m okay after dinner, I’ll just say I’m tired, because who wants to get into all that at the kitchen table, you know?\n\nSo… I guess I’m not giving you a yes-or-no. I’m sorry. I just don’t know how to say it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after eating more than you planned, and if so, how often does it happen?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a straight answer. I just… I don’t have a number for that. It’s not something I’ve sat down and counted, and when I try to think about it I start remembering random things, like whether my husband was home or if I had work the next morning, instead of the actual answer.\n\nAfter I eat more than I meant to, I mostly just feel ashamed and want the whole thing to be over. But saying exactly whether I made myself vomit, and how often… I’m not sure I can answer that clearly right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I keep getting stuck on the “made myself” part, I think. Like, after eating too much, I definitely feel upset and uncomfortable, and then I kind of go into my head about it.\n\nBut whether I’ve actually done that after those episodes… I don’t know. I know that sounds evasive. It’s just not something I have a clear answer for right now. My mind goes to all the circumstances around it—like if everyone was asleep, or if I had to get up early for work—instead of giving you the simple yes or no you’re asking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clear yes. I mean, after I eat more than I wanted to, I get really upset with myself, and then I start thinking about everything else—what I have to do the next morning, whether anyone noticed, whether I can just move on and pretend it didn’t happen.\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge you. I just don’t have it organized in my head like that, whether I’ve made myself vomit or how to describe it. It all gets kind of blurry when I try to talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever intentionally made yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. “Intentionally” makes me feel like I’m supposed to know exactly what I was deciding in the moment, and honestly, when I’ve eaten too much I’m usually just upset and sort of embarrassed.\n\nSometimes I think more about the situation around it—like whether I had to be at work early, or if I could just go to bed and pretend it didn’t happen—than about the actual question you’re asking. So I can’t honestly give you a clear yes or no right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything else to try to undo the effects of eating more than you planned, besides exercising more or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. When you say “undo,” I get what you mean, but my mind kind of goes to, like, trying to fix the day in general—cleaning up the kitchen, making plans for the next morning, telling myself I’ll be more normal tomorrow.\n\nI don’t know if there’s something specific you’re asking about besides eating less or moving more. I can’t think of anything clear right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used vomiting as a way to undo eating too much?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to say it. I hear “used vomiting,” and then I start thinking, well, was it that, or was I just feeling sick, or upset, or just wanting the whole thing to be over?\n\nIt’s like when I have a bad sales call and then the whole rest of the day feels ruined—I start trying to fix everything around it, even little things that don’t matter. So with eating too much, I know I get really distressed, but I can’t give you a clear answer that vomiting was the thing I was using to undo it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Like… it’s not every day, but for the last few months it’s been pretty regular—maybe once or twice a week I’ll have a night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day I kind of go into this “fix it” mode. I don’t really keep track of things that way, and honestly I get kind of embarrassed even talking about it. But I’ll be standing there tugging at my clothes and thinking about how I look instead of what I’m supposed to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve felt awful after, like too full and embarrassed, and then I’ll start cleaning the kitchen or checking emails because I don’t want to think about it. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m going to be more disciplined the next day, especially if I’ve got appointments or I have to dress up for work. But as far as making myself vomit… I don’t know, I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: Um… laxatives or diuretics? I don’t really… I’m not sure. I mean, I know what laxatives are, but diuretics, is that like water pills? My aunt used to talk about those all the time because her ankles would swell, and she’d keep them in one of those little pill organizers on the kitchen counter.\n\nFor me, after I eat too much, I mostly just feel uncomfortable and mad at myself, and then I start thinking about what I have to do the next morning—like whether I need to be in front of clients or if I can hide at my desk a little. I can’t say I’ve noticed a pattern with taking something like that afterward, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, and then you don't do anything to try to undo the effects of that eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… usually I do something, just not always right then. Like the next day I’ll tell myself I’m “making up for it,” you know? I’ll skip breakfast, and then lunch too sometimes, or I’ll make myself take this extra-long hike even if my legs are tired. \n\nAnd with softball conditioning, I’ll push harder than I probably need to, because in my head it balances things out somehow. I know that sounds a little… I don’t know, like a bargain I’m making with myself. But it doesn’t always feel like I’m doing something dramatic. It’s more like, “Okay, yesterday was bad, so today you have to be stricter.”\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you planned happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks lately. Like, not every single day or anything, but maybe one or two nights in a week where I look back and think, “That was way more than I meant to eat.”\n\nIt’s been going on for a few months, I think. I lose track a little because weeks blur together, especially with work and appointments and all that. But yeah, usually after one of those nights, the next day I’m trying to make up for it somehow, like skipping meals or making myself exercise longer than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: How often do you do something to make up for eating more than you planned, like skipping meals or exercising more?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I think. Like… it’s not every day, but for the last few months it’s been pretty regular—maybe once or twice a week I’ll have a night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day I kind of go into this “fix it” mode. I’ll skip breakfast, sometimes lunch, or I’ll make myself exercise longer than I planned. Like if I was only going to walk a bit, I’ll turn it into this whole long thing. It’s almost like I’m keeping score with myself, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. It’s not something I’ve really kept track of or talked about much, and I get embarrassed when it gets that specific.\n\nI guess when you ask it that directly, my mind kind of goes blank. Like I start thinking about whether I’m saying it “right,” which I know isn’t the point. I’m sorry—I might need you to ask it again a different way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, after those nights I feel awful and I’ll start thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then my brain jumps to all these rules about the next day.\n\nThe vomiting part… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep track of things that way, and honestly I get kind of embarrassed even talking about it. Like when my daughter asks if I’m okay after dinner, I’ll just say I’m tired, because who wants to get into all that at the kitchen table, you know?\n\nSo… I guess I’m not giving you a yes-or-no. I’m sorry. I just don’t know how to say it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after eating more than you planned, and if so, how often does it happen?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a straight answer. I just… I don’t have a number for that. It’s not something I’ve sat down and counted, and when I try to think about it I start remembering random things, like whether my husband was home or if I had work the next morning, instead of the actual answer.\n\nAfter I eat more than I meant to, I mostly just feel ashamed and want the whole thing to be over. But saying exactly whether I made myself vomit, and how often… I’m not sure I can answer that clearly right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I keep getting stuck on the “made myself” part, I think. Like, after eating too much, I definitely feel upset and uncomfortable, and then I kind of go into my head about it.\n\nBut whether I’ve actually done that after those episodes… I don’t know. I know that sounds evasive. It’s just not something I have a clear answer for right now. My mind goes to all the circumstances around it—like if everyone was asleep, or if I had to get up early for work—instead of giving you the simple yes or no you’re asking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clear yes. I mean, after I eat more than I wanted to, I get really upset with myself, and then I start thinking about everything else—what I have to do the next morning, whether anyone noticed, whether I can just move on and pretend it didn’t happen.\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge you. I just don’t have it organized in my head like that, whether I’ve made myself vomit or how to describe it. It all gets kind of blurry when I try to talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever intentionally made yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. “Intentionally” makes me feel like I’m supposed to know exactly what I was deciding in the moment, and honestly, when I’ve eaten too much I’m usually just upset and sort of embarrassed.\n\nSometimes I think more about the situation around it—like whether I had to be at work early, or if I could just go to bed and pretend it didn’t happen—than about the actual question you’re asking. So I can’t honestly give you a clear yes or no right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything else to try to undo the effects of eating more than you planned, besides exercising more or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. When you say “undo,” I get what you mean, but my mind kind of goes to, like, trying to fix the day in general—cleaning up the kitchen, making plans for the next morning, telling myself I’ll be more normal tomorrow.\n\nI don’t know if there’s something specific you’re asking about besides eating less or moving more. I can’t think of anything clear right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used vomiting as a way to undo eating too much?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to say it. I hear “used vomiting,” and then I start thinking, well, was it that, or was I just feeling sick, or upset, or just wanting the whole thing to be over?\n\nIt’s like when I have a bad sales call and then the whole rest of the day feels ruined—I start trying to fix everything around it, even little things that don’t matter. So with eating too much, I know I get really distressed, but I can’t give you a clear answer that vomiting was the thing I was using to undo it.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself vomit after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can put a number on that. When you say “make myself,” I get stuck there, because after I eat more than I meant to, everything feels sort of messy and I’m upset with myself, and then I start thinking about how I’m going to get through the next day or whether my clothes feel wrong, things like that.\n\nSo… frequency-wise, I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it in a way I could tell you, like, “this many times.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever vomited after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can’t say clearly, yes or no, the way you’re asking. There have been times after I ate more than I meant to where I felt really awful, like physically and mentally, and I just wanted the feeling to pass.\n\nBut then I start second-guessing what counts as “after,” or whether it was because I was upset or because my stomach felt off. It’s like I get tangled in the wording. I’m sorry—I know that’s not a straight answer. I just don’t have a clear one.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever vomited after eating more than you planned, even if it wasn't on purpose?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “not on purpose” makes it harder for me to sort out, because sometimes if I’ve eaten more than I planned, I feel so wound up and uncomfortable that everything kind of runs together.\n\nLike, I’ll be thinking about what I have to wear to work the next morning, or whether I’m going to feel bloated sitting in the car between appointments, and then I’m not really separating out what happened physically versus what I was feeling. So… I don’t know that I can honestly give you a clean yes or no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

When you say “make myself,” I get stuck there, because after I eat more than I meant to, everything feels sort of messy and I’m upset with myself, and then I start thinking about how I’m going to get through the next day or whether my clothes feel wrong, things like that.\n\nSo… frequency-wise, I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it in a way I could tell you, like, “this many times.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever vomited after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can’t say clearly, yes or no, the way you’re asking. There have been times after I ate more than I meant to where I felt really awful, like physically and mentally, and I just wanted the feeling to pass.\n\nBut then I start second-guessing what counts as “after,” or whether it was because I was upset or because my stomach felt off. It’s like I get tangled in the wording. I’m sorry—I know that’s not a straight answer. I just don’t have a clear one.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever vomited after eating more than you planned, even if it wasn't on purpose?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “not on purpose” makes it harder for me to sort out, because sometimes if I’ve eaten more than I planned, I feel so wound up and uncomfortable that everything kind of runs together.\n\nLike, I’ll be thinking about what I have to wear to work the next morning, or whether I’m going to feel bloated sitting in the car between appointments, and then I’m not really separating out what happened physically versus what I was feeling. So… I don’t know that I can honestly give you a clean yes or no.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever vomited after eating more than you planned, and was it something that you did on purpose?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / insurance_sales_agent / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wish I could give you a simple yes or no, but I keep getting hung up on the “on purpose” part. There have been times after eating more than I meant to where I felt awful and just wanted everything to stop feeling so uncomfortable, but when I try to say whether I actually made it happen, my mind kind of jumps around.

Like yesterday I was thinking about this while driving between clients, and then I started thinking about lunch and whether I should’ve packed something instead of buying something, and then it all just turns into this whole spiral. So… I don’t know. I’m not trying to avoid the question, I just don’t feel sure enough to answer it clearly.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.4, F50.5\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess it’s my eating, mostly. I work in insurance sales, so by the time I get home some days I’m just… worn out, you know? And if I’ve been really “good” all day, like keeping things strict, then some evenings I end up eating way more than I planned. It’ll start with dinner, and then somehow it’s cereal, crackers, leftovers, sweets—just sort of one thing after another over a couple hours. I keep telling myself I’ll get back on track tomorrow, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight when you think about these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not always in a big dramatic way, but it gets in my head. Like if I’m getting ready for a client luncheon or one of those leadership meetings and my waistband feels tight, I start thinking I look sloppy, like people won’t take me as seriously. Which is silly, because I know I’m still doing my job fine, and honestly half the time everyone’s just worried about their own numbers or whatever. But I’ll be standing there tugging at my clothes and thinking about how I look instead of what I’m supposed to say.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve felt awful after, like too full and embarrassed, and then I’ll start cleaning the kitchen or checking emails because I don’t want to think about it. Sometimes I’ll tell myself I’m going to be more disciplined the next day, especially if I’ve got appointments or I have to dress up for work. But as far as making myself vomit… I don’t know, I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: Um… laxatives or diuretics? I don’t really… I’m not sure. I mean, I know what laxatives are, but diuretics, is that like water pills? My aunt used to talk about those all the time because her ankles would swell, and she’d keep them in one of those little pill organizers on the kitchen counter.\n\nFor me, after I eat too much, I mostly just feel uncomfortable and mad at myself, and then I start thinking about what I have to do the next morning—like whether I need to be in front of clients or if I can hide at my desk a little. I can’t say I’ve noticed a pattern with taking something like that afterward, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times where you eat a large amount of food in a short period, and then you don't do anything to try to undo the effects of that eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… usually I do something, just not always right then. Like the next day I’ll tell myself I’m “making up for it,” you know? I’ll skip breakfast, and then lunch too sometimes, or I’ll make myself take this extra-long hike even if my legs are tired. \n\nAnd with softball conditioning, I’ll push harder than I probably need to, because in my head it balances things out somehow. I know that sounds a little… I don’t know, like a bargain I’m making with myself. But it doesn’t always feel like I’m doing something dramatic. It’s more like, “Okay, yesterday was bad, so today you have to be stricter.”\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you planned happen?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I guess most weeks lately. Like, not every single day or anything, but maybe one or two nights in a week where I look back and think, “That was way more than I meant to eat.”\n\nIt’s been going on for a few months, I think. I lose track a little because weeks blur together, especially with work and appointments and all that. But yeah, usually after one of those nights, the next day I’m trying to make up for it somehow, like skipping meals or making myself exercise longer than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: How often do you do something to make up for eating more than you planned, like skipping meals or exercising more?\n\nPatient: Most weeks, I think. Like… it’s not every day, but for the last few months it’s been pretty regular—maybe once or twice a week I’ll have a night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day I kind of go into this “fix it” mode. I’ll skip breakfast, sometimes lunch, or I’ll make myself exercise longer than I planned. Like if I was only going to walk a bit, I’ll turn it into this whole long thing. It’s almost like I’m keeping score with myself, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. It’s not something I’ve really kept track of or talked about much, and I get embarrassed when it gets that specific.\n\nI guess when you ask it that directly, my mind kind of goes blank. Like I start thinking about whether I’m saying it “right,” which I know isn’t the point. I’m sorry—I might need you to ask it again a different way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, after those nights I feel awful and I’ll start thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then my brain jumps to all these rules about the next day.\n\nThe vomiting part… I’m not sure. I don’t really keep track of things that way, and honestly I get kind of embarrassed even talking about it. Like when my daughter asks if I’m okay after dinner, I’ll just say I’m tired, because who wants to get into all that at the kitchen table, you know?\n\nSo… I guess I’m not giving you a yes-or-no. I’m sorry. I just don’t know how to say it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after eating more than you planned, and if so, how often does it happen?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I know you’re trying to get a straight answer. I just… I don’t have a number for that. It’s not something I’ve sat down and counted, and when I try to think about it I start remembering random things, like whether my husband was home or if I had work the next morning, instead of the actual answer.\n\nAfter I eat more than I meant to, I mostly just feel ashamed and want the whole thing to be over. But saying exactly whether I made myself vomit, and how often… I’m not sure I can answer that clearly right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after one of these episodes of eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I keep getting stuck on the “made myself” part, I think. Like, after eating too much, I definitely feel upset and uncomfortable, and then I kind of go into my head about it.\n\nBut whether I’ve actually done that after those episodes… I don’t know. I know that sounds evasive. It’s just not something I have a clear answer for right now. My mind goes to all the circumstances around it—like if everyone was asleep, or if I had to get up early for work—instead of giving you the simple yes or no you’re asking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can give you a clear yes. I mean, after I eat more than I wanted to, I get really upset with myself, and then I start thinking about everything else—what I have to do the next morning, whether anyone noticed, whether I can just move on and pretend it didn’t happen.\n\nSo… I’m not trying to dodge you. I just don’t have it organized in my head like that, whether I’ve made myself vomit or how to describe it. It all gets kind of blurry when I try to talk about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever intentionally made yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure how to answer that cleanly. “Intentionally” makes me feel like I’m supposed to know exactly what I was deciding in the moment, and honestly, when I’ve eaten too much I’m usually just upset and sort of embarrassed.\n\nSometimes I think more about the situation around it—like whether I had to be at work early, or if I could just go to bed and pretend it didn’t happen—than about the actual question you’re asking. So I can’t honestly give you a clear yes or no right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything else to try to undo the effects of eating more than you planned, besides exercising more or skipping meals?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. When you say “undo,” I get what you mean, but my mind kind of goes to, like, trying to fix the day in general—cleaning up the kitchen, making plans for the next morning, telling myself I’ll be more normal tomorrow.\n\nI don’t know if there’s something specific you’re asking about besides eating less or moving more. I can’t think of anything clear right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used vomiting as a way to undo eating too much?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to say it. I hear “used vomiting,” and then I start thinking, well, was it that, or was I just feeling sick, or upset, or just wanting the whole thing to be over?\n\nIt’s like when I have a bad sales call and then the whole rest of the day feels ruined—I start trying to fix everything around it, even little things that don’t matter. So with eating too much, I know I get really distressed, but I can’t give you a clear answer that vomiting was the thing I was using to undo it.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself vomit after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can put a number on that. When you say “make myself,” I get stuck there, because after I eat more than I meant to, everything feels sort of messy and I’m upset with myself, and then I start thinking about how I’m going to get through the next day or whether my clothes feel wrong, things like that.\n\nSo… frequency-wise, I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it in a way I could tell you, like, “this many times.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever vomited after eating more than you planned?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can’t say clearly, yes or no, the way you’re asking. Like… it’s not every day, but for the last few months it’s been pretty regular—maybe once or twice a week I’ll have a night where I eat way more than I meant to.\n\nAnd then the next day I kind of go into this “fix it” mode.
